php2shell by Calif Research

The year was 2004. The iPhone was yet to be invented and our very own Stefan Esser was still the PHP security guy. Read the scene2004. Stefan searches for forbidden knowledge and discovers an ancient

10/21 13:58 · 🔖 19
📄 本文を読む
PHP >= 5.4.0 RCE
PHP can leave dangling pointers/state when an allocation would exceed memory_limit, or another fatal error interrupts native code before its normal cleanup completes. Request shutdown may then free memory that surviving state still references.
To reach an exploitable state we found two general OOM/memory_limit based fatal producers and three different interruptible points in the interpreter that can leave a stale/consumable state that allows leaking memory and remote code execution.
The earliest affected path we found dates to PHP 5.4.0; the available exploitation paths depend on the PHP version and SAPI but are always exploitable under default configurations.
We confirmed pre-auth RCE on the following PHP apps as well as arbitrary PHP scripts in some instances.
- WordPress 7.1.2
- Drupal 11.4.3
- Joomla 6.1.4
- MediaWiki 1.46.2
- Roundcube 1.7.4
- Magento 2.4.9
- phpMyAdmin 5.2.3
- Nextcloud 35.0.1
- Laravel 13.11
- Symfony 8.1
- WHMCS
Here is a demo video of the exploits running:
You said OOM-fatal?
Right. We should explain from the start.
My first step of finding if any long-lost siblings of Stefan's 2004 bug were still lingering in the codebase was to look for
ways to exceed the memory_limit that allows us to throw a controllable fatal. I asked
my LLM companion for help.
The first useful finding was a way to bypass the smaller post_max_size barrier that normally
prevents a request from delivering enough memory pressure to reach memory_limit.
PHP compares post_max_size
with the declared Content-Length before automatic POST parsing begins, but it
does not enforce the same limit against the bytes it actually consumes. Apache can therefore pass a chunked HTTP/1.1 or lengthless
HTTP/2 multipart request with no meaningful declared length. A configured input-decompression filter creates the same mismatch between
the compressed length and the decoded stream.
The second useful finding was a way to exhaust memory_limit
with a multipart request that remains below post_max_size. An RFC1867 file-upload part with
filename="" contains no file data, and PHP does not count it toward
max_file_uploads. PHP still creates all six corresponding
$_FILES entries: name, full_path,
type, tmp_name, error, and size.
When the form field name is nested, such as files[a][b][c]...,
PHP must create that hierarchy separately for each of the six entries. One short empty-upload record expands into many native
HashTables, Buckets and string-key allocations. Hundreds of these records can
occupy more than the default 128 MiB PHP heap while their complete HTTP body remains below the default 8 MiB
post_max_size. This lets us make a chosen later allocation hit
memory_limit.
But what does an OOM buy you, really?
Now that we can cause OOM/fatal on demand, the question is what can we interrupt that leaves the process in an exploitable state.
We asked our audit buddy to go over ZendMM allocation behavior, fatal reporting, and request
shutdown for interesting points which may be vulnerable to unexpected interruptions. During that source review our agent noticed this ordering
in _zend_hash_str_add_or_update_i:
idx = ht->nNumUsed++;
ht->nNumOfElements++;
p = ht->arData + idx;
p->key = zend_string_init(str, len, persistent); /* may zend_bailout() */
p->h = ZSTR_H(p->key) = h;
ZVAL_COPY_VALUE(&p->val, pData);
PHP increments both nNumUsed and
nNumOfElements before fully initializing the new bucket. Increasing
nNumUsed makes the
bucket visible to teardown, even if the subsequent key allocation fatals as can be seen in the relevant branch of
zend_array_destroy():
for (p = ht->arData; p != ht->arData + ht->nNumUsed; p++) {
i_zval_ptr_dtor(&p->val);
if (p->key) {
zend_string_release(p->key);
}
}
This allows us to ensure that local control flow never reaches the
key, hash, zval or collision-chain assignments. But the count remains incremented,
so teardown later consumes stale allocation bytes as a live bucket:
increment live count
|
v
Bucket becomes teardown-visible
|
v
allocate owned key ----fatal----+
| |
v v
initialize Bucket assignments skipped
| |
v v
valid table teardown trusts live count
|
v
stale Bucket consumption
My agent then traced the source root to automatic nested POST parsing and discovered that mod_php shutdown consumes the malformed table.
The vulnerable _zend_hash_str_add_or_update_i
is reached through normal request-variable registration:
URL-encoded POST root[x]=v
php

息子に何を教えるべきか?What shall I teach my son?
10/20 17:25 · ▲ 468 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
What Shall I Teach My Son?
What is the purpose of education in the age of AGI?
October 19, 2026
Yesterday, the title The End of Education by Neil Postman called to me from my bookshelf, whispering that it might be the right book at the right time. It was. Published in 1995, it was surprisingly appropriate balm for the soul of a confused programmer and soon-to-be father.
My wife and I are expecting our first child. My economically valuable skillset – programming – suddenly feels less valuable. What shall I teach my son? And to what end?
Mr. Edison says
That the radio will supplant the teacher.
Already one may learn languages by means
Of Victrola records.
The moving picture will visualize
What the radio fails to get across.
Teachers will be relegated to the backwoods.
With fire-horses,
And long-haired women;
Or, perhaps shown in museums.
Education will become a matter
Of pressing the button.
Perhaps I can get a position at the switchboard.
– Quoted in The End of Education by Neil Postman, quoting Teachers and Machines: The Use of Technology Since 1920 by Larry Cuban
In Edison’s own words:
Books will soon be obsolete in the public schools… Our school system will be completely changed inside of ten years.
– Thomas Edison, The New York Dramatic Mirror, 1913
Back in 2013, I boldly predicted that MOOCs would upend education. Ah, my past self, you sweet summer child.
Nassim Nicholas Taleb has helped me appreciate the staying power of staying power, sometimes called The Lindy Effect. Merely staying alive implies some anti-fragility to adapt and survive. Consider that Harvard, Yale, and Princeton were founded to educate the clergy. From The End of Education:
In the Western world, beginning in the thirteenth century and for five hundred years afterward, that God was sufficient justification for the founding of institutions of learning, from grammar schools, where children were taught to read the Bible, to great universities, where men were trained to be ministers of God.
Schools survived what happened to religion. It should be no surprise that they’ve survived the radio, motion pictures, and the internet.
We typically confuse why something claims it exists, ie to glorify God, vs the forces that actually perpetuate it, which are subtler, we’d prefer not spoken aloud, and more prosaic, ie endowments, prestige, and needing someone to watch one’s kids.
It has been a surprisingly good bet that schools will continue to exist in roughly their current form for many years to come. Ironically, there also seems to be some staying power to technologists boldly (and so far incorrectly) doubting the staying power of schools! I myself have contributed to this grand tradition multiple times, and find myself at risk of doing it yet again.
Surely this time must finally be different, right? We have captured intelligence in a bottle, and you’re telling me that “Ok class, turn to page 17” and “Does anyone know how to solve for x” are still going to be around in the 2030s?
There are many shifting purposes to education, which underpin how it has survived this long. As the times change, so do its purposes shift and adapt, such as:
- become a good person
- become a good citizen
- earn a living
- make the world better
- learn basic facts
I once tried to come up with a single-word purpose for education, and I landed upon the word “resourceful”, because I reasoned that if you “were able to use arbitrary resources to uncover and solve problems”, you had no more need for schooling. In the age of Google, email, and the internet, a person was educated when they could wield them. But now I see my vision of resourcefulness perfected in the AI agent and its tool loop. Maybe resourcefulness will go the way of the Dewey Decimal System which in turn supplanted memorization and an oral tradition.
Imagine education as a table held up by its many purposes. I didn’t realize how centrally important “the ability to earn a living” was for me until it started to wobble. A big reason is because I’m looking for a more objective arbiter of “good” in education. Education’s own tests and measures can feel stifling. The market offered a counterpoint. Steve Jobs and Bill Gates were college dropouts, but the market deemed them good. Now that I think of it, apparently Einstein was a mediocre student, but his contributions to science were his way out. With recent advances in AI mathematics, both of these paths to glory are under fire. Clara Collier makes similar points in her exceptional essay “After Work, We’ll Have Each Other.”
If AI takes our ability to contribute, what’s left for our children?
Neil Postman offers a few suggestions for positive purposes for education that I found heartening, not mainly for their content, but for the fact that it’s possible to make up new purposes for education and advocate for them. My favorite suggestions of Postman’s are:
- Spaceship Earth – we’re all in this together; let’s take care of each other and our planet
- The American Experi
HNコラム
ディープウォーター・ホライズンから16年、深海生物に回復の兆しはほとんど見られないSixteen years after Deepwater Horizon, deep sea life shows few signs of recovery
10/20 15:59 · ▲ 317 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Sixteen years after an explosion on the Deepwater Horizon oil rig unleashed more than 4 million barrels of oil into the Gulf of Mexico, life in the deep sea has yet to recover.
A new study finds that in the first few years after the 2010 disaster, populations of octopus, squid, krill, shrimp, and lanternfish declined by at least 60 percent. The research, based on acoustic surveys and thousands of netted samples across a broad swath of the Gulf of Mexico, found little evidence that life in the deep sea has bounced back.
The study, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, described the losses as “the fastest and most severe” decline of animal life in the ocean layer that extends from about 600 feet deep to more than two miles down. While researchers stopped short of concluding that the oil spill had caused the declines, they said it was “the most parsimonious explanation.”
Since the disaster, scientists have found oil-related compounds in the flesh of squid and octopus and in fish eggs deep underwater, contamination that they say can impair reproduction and development or lead to an early death. Lingering pollution may make it harder for deep-sea fish that are guided by their sense of smell to find mates.
Beyond the drop in population numbers, the new study also found some declines in genetic diversity. The risk, authors say, is that populations may not harbor the genetic traits needed to survive in a rapidly changing world.

HN政治
パリ協定の1.5℃目標はもはや達成不可能The 1.5°C goal of the Paris Agreement is no longer viable
10/21 09:07 · ▲ 603 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
The target set in 2015 by the Paris Agreement—to limit global warming to 1.5°C by 2100—has become unachievable within the span of a single decade, the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) notes in its 2026 Emissions Gap Report.
“The opportunity to bridge the 1.5°C emissions gap with ‘no or limited’ overshoot has been lost,” states the report, titled “The Point of Return.”
Exceeding the 1.5°C threshold above pre-industrial levels is now imminent and inevitable.
New goal: not exceed 2°C, then bring the temperature back down to 1.5°C
“Every additional fraction of a degree and additional year of warming above 1.5°C raises the risk of irreversible changes including in ice sheets, permafrost carbon, ocean circulation and coral systems, along with devastating human impacts,” the report states.
The urgent task is to ensure that global warming remains below the 2°C threshold, with the aim of returning to 1.5°C by the end of the century. This is the only option left, according to UNEP.
Highly inadequate climate plans
To achieve this, global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions would need to be reduced by 18% by 2030, 45% by 2035, and 69% by 2040, relative to 2025 levels.
The current trajectory falls far short of this. Even assuming that the commitments made by governments—known as nationally determined contributions (NDCs)—are fully implemented, emissions would only be reduced by 5% to 8% by 2030 and by 11% to 15% by 2035, compared to 2025 levels.
The recommended efforts are “close to 6 per cent every single year until 2035,” declares the UN Secretary General.
How can this be achieved?
The UN Chief outlined the levers for action available to humanity:
“The emissions gap is now a leadership gap. And the world is running out of time and excuses to close it.
Governments must go much further and faster. Accelerate clean energy and electrification across every sector.
Adopt credible roadmaps to transition away from fossil fuels – with clear timelines and protection for affected workers and communities.
Invest in grids and storage. Improve energy efficiency. Cut methane emissions. Halt and reverse deforestation.”
What are the specific targets?
The report indicates that by 2035, the capacity of renewable energy sources must triple compared to 2025 levels. Furthermore, the phase-out of fossil fuels requires reducing the respective shares of coal, oil, and gas in energy production by 55%, 15%, and 5% by 2035.
By 2040, reductions of at least 75%, 30%, and 35% would be necessary.
Large-scale operations to remove CO2 from the atmosphere will also be imperative to return to the 1.5°C threshold—or even lower.
UNEP estimates that 320 gigatons of CO2 —equivalent to eight times the emissions recorded in 2024— must be removed from the atmosphere by the end of the century. This implies biological solutions, such as reforestation, as well as technological approaches involving the compression, mineralisation, and underground storage of CO2.
Reversing the trend remains possible
Accelerating the momentum is still possible, according to UNEP, given the significant progress made since 2015.
“Since the adoption of the Paris Agreement, global renewable electricity capacity has grown three times faster than anticipated, with solar PV build-out increasing seven times faster”, states UNEP Chief Inger Andersen.
“Total energy supply from coal and oil has grown slower than projected. Climate policies have delivered real emission reductions across the world and across sectors. Several G20 members have shown sustained emission reductions, and some are aligning policies with net-zero pledges.”
The progress made remains insufficient, particularly with regard to fossil fuels and deforestation.
Because the climate knows no borders, limiting any overshoot of 1.5°C as much as possible requires action across the entire economy and on a global scale.

「No Kings」アカウント停止が示す、Zuckerbergから拡声器を借りるべきでない理由The No Kings Takedown Is Why You Shouldn't Rent Your Megaphone from Zuck
10/21 14:01 · ▲ 474 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Accident Or Not, The No Kings Takedown Is Why You Shouldn’t Rent Your Megaphone From Zuck
from the no-kings-is-about-more-than-politicians dept
This past weekend was the latest in an ongoing series of (massive) “No Kings” protests held around the country (and in some cases around the globe). The latest one was part of a “get out the (early) vote” campaign to make sure people are voting in the upcoming midterm elections. And, in the middle of it, suddenly it was reported that the No Kings Instagram account was suspended for about an hour, with admin access locked out for a few more hours. Many supporters of the protests immediately suspected foul play, highlighting that Mark Zuckerberg has gone in hard in supporting Trump over the past couple of years. So, the implication was that Zuck must be fiddling the dials to help the guy he supports in the White House. For a variety of reasons, this is unlikely to be true.
I’ll explain some of those reasons towards the end of this article just to be thorough, but the point of this article is to highlight the argument that Will Robinson and Tim Chambers recently made in response to this, which is that it doesn’t even matter if it was done on purpose or not. Even if you believe that Zuckerberg and Trump were laughing their heads off and jointly pressing the button to silence the No Kings account, the real problem was that this was possible in the first place.
Our response should be the same either way.
Robinson and Chambers focus on the very practical lessons for anyone engaged in digital organizing, which is how to prepare for the possibility that a centralized provider takes down your account. And their advice is good and practical (read the whole thing!): make sure you have communication channels that a third party can’t easily switch off, set up accounts on open platforms, and have a playbook on how to respond (mostly getting out the word) if your account gets shut down.
But it’s also a reminder of the larger point that I have been harping on for some time about the importance of decentralization and user control over their online experiences. Centralized systems create a natural gravity that leads to control over your speech. My friend Danny O’Brien (who also helped inspire Techdirt’s very existence) wrote a few years ago about how decentralization is not an end in and of itself, but rather part of the means to guarantee we have “cognitive liberty.”
When we hand over the control of our speech to a third party, with no ability to exit (with all of our content and connections intact) we have given up a fundamental element of our liberty. O’Brien describes it here:
Right now, if you wrote a note to yourself and put it on your personal computer’s hard drive, you could say whatever you want, draw whatever you want. If it’s in the cloud, you don’t have that guarantee: even if you kept it in your own Google Drive or DropBox, it could be found wanting, and deleted. I don’t know about you, but my notes are my memory, most of the time. My search engine searches are as much me talking to myself about my worries and interests, in a way that I would rarely disclose even to my closest companion.
[….]
Our own consciousness cannot be rented from others, or temporarily conceded to us, with built-in police or backdoors or hidden ad men. We need to seize the means of computation, and that means ejecting all of these interlopers, and relocating it back into the personal domain we control: whether that’s physically, or by using tools like encryption and zero-knowledge proofs to preserve our control when our data and processing power sits on others’ hardware.
That’s the pyramid of digital rights for me: a firm foundation of decentralized, user-controlled technology, giving us broader cognitive liberty, internal privacy, freedom of self-expression, and freedom of self-determination. On top of that solid ground, we can build a society that’s free and fair. And then we can have the ability and freedom to self-reflect, to talk, and to plot our better shared future together, free at last in our digital environment.
Achieving that kind of freedom for “cognitive liberty” requires us to recognize the threats of centralized systems. And Meta shutting down the “No Kings” Insta account is one such example no matter if it was on purpose or an accident. This was the very thinking behind my original Protocols, Not Platforms paper in the first place. If you hand control of your digital spaces to someone else, they get to decide whether those spaces exist at all.
And they might be bad at it, whether through incompetence or malice.
And sometimes that means your account gets shut down — whether for good reasons or bad. Many people seem to not want to grapple with this issue. They say “the people are there,” and that is true, but only because the “people” have agreed to give Mark Zuckerberg and whoever else that power.
Unfortunately, most of the “solutions” to this problem that people are suggesting are s

EAがBioWareを閉鎖へEA is shutting down BioWare
10/21 17:29 · ▲ 409 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
BioWare, the legendary studio behind RPGs like Mass Effect, Dragon Age, and Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic, is shutting down, the company confirmed in a statement. Alongside the closure, Electronic Arts has chosen not to continue development on the next Mass Effect game that was in the works, according to an announcement sent to EA staff.
EA is shutting down BioWare
Amid a reported 400 job cuts, the legendary studio is closing.
The company is also laying off around 400 staffers, Bloomberg reports, including around 50 at BioWare. The changes follow EA being taken private by an investor group led by Saudi Arabia’s Public Investment Fund, Silver Lake, and Affinity Partners in a $55 billion deal that closed in August. Other major gaming companies have made sweeping changes this year as well, including Xbox’s “reset” that will include 3,200 cuts, Bungie’s “significant” layoffs, and Fortnite maker Epic Games laying off over 1,000 staff.
“BioWare is a legendary studio that helped define the role-playing genre, and we do not take this decision lightly,” Cam Weber, EA president and chief studios officer, and David Tinson, EA president and COO, said in the announcement.
Here is the full message that BioWare shared on social media:
For more than 30 years, BioWare has been more than a studio. It has been a home for storytellers, developers, and the players who brought our worlds to life.
Baldur’s Gate. Neverwinter Nights. Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic. Dragon Age. Mass Effect. Anthem.
Now, after more than three decades of shared adventures, BioWare is closing and early development on the next Mass Effect project is ending.
There is nothing easy about writing those words. Our focus right now is on our incredibly talented team who put their hearts into these games, and supporting one another through this transition.
What generations of BioWare built together is something to celebrate and remember. These games became so much more because you played them, debated your choices, fell in love with the characters, and made these worlds your own.
From all of us at BioWare, thank you for being the most meaningful part of our story.
While the end of BioWare will shock many gamers who fondly remember its hits, the company hasn’t been as successful in recent years. 2017’s Mass Effect: Andromeda was widely seen as a misstep for that franchise, it took a big bet on live service games in 2019 with Anthem that didn’t pan out, and EA laid off some BioWare staff in 2025 after Dragon Age: The Veilguard missed sales expectations.
There is no word yet on the fate of Amazon’s Mass Effect TV series. Last we heard, Amazon’s head of TV was close to giving it the green light, but had reportedly requested rewrites to make the show “more appealing to non-gamers.” Amazon MGM Studios spokesperson Nate Kirtman declined to comment. EA spokesperson Justin Higgs said he had “Nothing to share on that at this point.”
In EA’s company-wide announcement, Weber and Tinson outlined several other projects in development and changes to EA’s roadmap. The executives say that Respawn and DICE will be collaborating on “a future project,” which Bloomberg says is “likely” a new Star Wars: Battlefront game. The company is still working Marvel’s Iron Man and a new Star Wars Jedi title. EA is making a “brand-new, social-first open-world experience” football game that it calls “Project Gridiron” and is also in pre-production on a new Fight Night boxing title (which was teased yesterday). For The Sims, the company is pausing development on “Project Lotus” and focusing on “Project Evergreen.” And for its live-service Skate, EA is “making changes to the studio” to “deliver on a very clear roadmap for our community.”
Here is the full message from Weber and Tinson at Electronic Arts:
Hi Team,
First, thank you.
Through every major moment we’ve faced as a company, your commitment to creating awesome games and experiences for our players continues to be impressive. Together, we’ve built epic worlds and global communities that are part of the lives of hundreds of millions of people.
Today, we’re the number one publisher in Western HD gaming. That’s an extraordinary position to lead from, and one we should be proud of. Yet our industry is changing quickly. Players have more choices than ever for their time and attention, competition is intensifying, and some of our biggest opportunities for growth are beyond the markets, platforms, and business models where EA has traditionally been the strongest.
That’s why, over the past several months, we’ve taken a hard look at our business, how we operate, and what it will take to lead in this next era of interactive entertainment. We’ve challenged assumptions, confronted hard realities, imagined new possibilities, and made deliberate choices in order to deliver exceptional experiences for our players and to drive sustained growth.
We believe our best creative work happens when we’re clear about what matters most for our playe
